{"parentUuid":"32a1ef44-06b6-49a4-874e-5ffb30123384","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"748ebfe1-b95a-4d93-8f5f-b428a8da9b41","agentId":"a4395ccb724875f20","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01XtjPJEwxxHGGqYDQqBZTJK","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# Japanese Temple/Shrine Business Models - Detailed Research\n2\t\n3\t> Research Date: 2026-05-19\n4\t> Purpose: 봉은사 구 웨딩홀 리뉴얼 프로젝트 참고자료\n5\t\n6\t---\n7\t\n8\t## 1. Koyasan Shukubo Network (Mt. Koya, Wakayama)\n9\t\n10\t### Top-Rated Shukubo Temples\n11\t\n12\t**Eko-in (恵光院)**\n13\t- Koyasan's most popular temple lodging, famous for Goma fire ritual\n14\t- Room types:\n15\t  - **Gachirin (月輪) Suite**: 100m², largest suite in all Koyasan shukubo. Opened March 2022. Features spacious bedroom, private semi-open-air bath, garden view. Price: ~$712/night average (luxury tier, exact JPY varies by season; peak autumn can exceed ¥50,000+ for standard rooms)\n16\t  - **Kongo Jr. Suite**: 60m², opened April 2024. Japanese-style room + bedroom + veranda\n17\t  - **Tenjiku Jr. Suite**: 65m², living room + Japanese room + bedroom + 15m² private garden\n18\t  - **Hisui Deluxe Room**: 50m², bedroom + Japanese room, panoramic town view, private bath\n19\t  - Standard rooms: tatami + futon, shared facilities. From ~$215/night\n20\t- Experiences: Morning religious service, Goma fire ritual, ajikan meditation, sutra copying\n21\t- Public baths: 16:00-22:00 and 06:00-09:00\n22\t\n23\t**Fukuchi-in (福智院)**\n24\t- Distinguished by having its own natural hot spring onsen (rare among shukubo)\n25\t- Traditional Japanese garden designed by Shigemori Mirei (重森三玲), one of Japan's most famous garden designers\n26\t- Shojin ryori meals included in stay\n27\t- Japanese-style rooms with garden views\n28\t\n29\t**Shojoshin-in (清浄心院)**\n30\t- Founded in Tencho era (822-83), one of the oldest temples on Mt. Koya (3 years older than Kongobuji)\n31\t- Prime location: right at the entrance to Okunoin path\n32\t- Features: \"Hanare\" private guest residence with private bath, Japanese gardens, pond\n33\t- Fire ceremony daily at 13:00\n34\t- Morning service followed by temple treasures tour (in Japanese)\n35\t- Guests describe dinner as \"10+ vegetarian dishes, absolutely fantastic\"\n36\t\n37\t### Shojin Ryori (Temple Cuisine) Details\n38\t\n39\t**Course Structure:**\n40\t- Deluxe: 3 courses, ~12 items\n41\t- Standard: 2 courses, ~10 items\n42\t- Simple: 2 courses, ~9 items\n43\t\n44\t**Five Principles:**\n45\t- Five methods: boiling, baking, steaming, frying, raw\n46\t- Five colors: red, yellow, green, white, black\n47\t- Five flavors: sweet, sour, spicy, bitter, salty\n48\t\n49\t**Signature Dishes:**\n50\t- **Goma Tofu (胡麻豆腐)**: Most iconic. Made with kuzuko (arrowroot powder) + ground sesame (not soy)\n51\t- **Shojin Hanazukushi**: Served in flower basket; shiitake shaped like mackerel, persimmon-leaf wrapped sushi, Koya vegetarian meatloaf\n52\t- Typical dinner spread: tofu varieties, tempura, hot pot, fresh-cut vegetables, mountain plants, pickled vegetables, soups, rice\n53\t- All strictly vegan: no meat, fish, eggs, or pungent alliums (garlic, onion)\n54\t\n55\t**Lunch Experience (Eko-in):**\n56\t- Available to non-staying visitors via \"More Koyasan\" booking\n57\t- Shojin ryori lunch experience bookable through byfood.com\n58\t\n59\t### Morning Prayer Ceremony\n60\t\n61\t- **Eko-in schedule**: Religious service at 7:00 AM daily in main hall, followed by Goma (Homa) Fire Ritual at 7:30 AM at the Bishamondo\n62\t- **General pattern**: Most shukubo hold morning prayers 6:00-7:00 AM\n63\t- **Duration**: ~30-45 minutes for service, additional 15-20 minutes for fire ritual\n64\t- **What happens**: Sutra chanting, incense offering, fire ritual (at temples offering Goma). Guests may participate or observe\n65\t- Not all temples perform fire rituals; Eko-in is one of the few that does\n66\t\n67\t### Okunoin Night Cemetery Tour\n68\t\n69\t- **Operator**: Awesome Tours (official licensed tour)\n70\t- **Check-in**: 13:00-17:00 and 18:30-19:00\n71\t- **Departure**: 19:00-19:15\n72\t- **End time**: 20:20 at Okunoin Gokusho\n73\t- **Duration**: ~90 minutes walking tour\n74\t- **Price**: ¥5,000 per person (from March 11, 2025; previously ¥4,500)\n75\t- **Language**: Always 2 English-speaking guides available\n76\t- **Content**: Walk through Japan's largest historical cemetery, stories of Kobo Daishi, Shingon Esoteric Buddhism teachings, cemetery legends, monk daily life, Koyasan history, notable tombs\n77\t- **Booking**: Separate from shukubo stay; advance reservation required\n78\t- **Atmosphere**: 200,000+ tombstones, ancient cedar trees, stone lanterns, described as \"unforgettable\" and \"atmospheric\"\n79\t\n80\t### Foreign Visitor Demographics\n81\t\n82\t**Japan-wide context (2024-2025):**\n83\t- Record 42.7M international visitors to Japan in 2025 (+15.8% YoY)\n84\t- Total spending: ~USD 60B (36.6% hotels, 27% shopping, 21.9% F&B)\n85\t- Top source markets: South Korea (9.5M), China (9.1M), Taiwan (6.8M)\n86\t- 35% solo travelers in 2024, 97% of solo travelers aged 18-28\n87\t- 70% of visitors stay in 3 major metro areas; 30% visit regional destinations\n88\t\n89\t**Koyasan-specific patterns:**\n90\t- Strong Western European and Australian visitor base (historically popular with backpackers since 1990s)\n91\t- Growing Asian visitor segment post-COVID\n92\t- Tour groups from France, UK, Australia particularly prominent\n93\t- Specific Koyasan demographics not publicly reported\n94\t\n95\t### Seasonal Occupancy Patterns\n96\t\n97\t- **Cherry Blossom**: Late April to early May (Koyasan blooms later than lowlands)\n98\t- **Summer Peak**: Late July-August (school groups flood Koyasan)\n99\t- **Autumn Foliage**: Peak around first week of November - **highest demand period**\n100\t  - Prices surge significantly; standard rooms can exceed ¥50,000/night\n101\t  - Must book 6-12 months in advance for preferred temples\n102\t- **Winter**: Lowest occupancy, snow-covered landscape, unique atmosphere\n103\t- **New Year**: Brief spike for Hatsumode visitors\n104\t\n105\t### Koyasan Shukubo Association (1958)\n106\t\n107\t- **Legal entity**: Corporation established 1958\n108\t- **Members**: 51 shukubo (temple stay facilities)\n109\t- **Registration**: Licensed travel agency in Wakayama Prefecture\n110\t- **Operations**:\n111\t  - 3 tourist information centers: Central, Ichinohashi, Nakanohashi\n112\t  - Official online booking system (koyasan-shukubo.net)\n113\t  - \"More Koyasan\" pre-bookable experience platform\n114\t  - Souvenir/book sales, cafe operations\n115\t  - Electric bicycle and GPS guide device rentals\n116\t- **Marketing**: Operates bilingual (JP/EN) websites, partnerships with OTAs (Booking.com, Agoda, Jalan, etc.)\n117\t\n118\t---\n119\t\n120\t## 2. Ninnaji Temple Luxury Stay (Kyoto)\n121\t\n122\t### \"Iroha Nihon\" Program\n123\t\n124\t- **Organizer**: Nippon Foundation (日本財団)\n125\t- **Mission**: Communicate \"the soul of Japan\" through cultural experiences at historic sites\n126\t- **Launch at Ninnaji**: Spring 2018\n127\t- **Structure**: Foundation subsidizes renovation; temple operates accommodation; revenue partially funds cultural preservation\n128\t\n129\t### Accommodation - Shorin-an (松林庵)\n130\t\n131\t- **Building history**: Former residence of Hisatomi family (samurai/physicians attached to Ninnaji)\n132\t- **How it came to be**: Donated structure, moved to temple grounds, fell into disrepair. Young temple staff created a plan and applied to Iroha Nihon program\n133\t- **Renovation cost**: ¥157 million total; **80% covered by Nippon Foundation subsidy**\n134\t- **Design**: Mix of traditional and contemporary Japanese styles\n135\t- **Features**: Traditional tearoom, private gardens, raised futon beds, lofty duvets, locally crafted furnishings, modern bathroom, A/C, Wi-Fi\n136\t- **Capacity**: Maximum 5 guests per night, 1 party only\n137\t\n138\t### The ¥1,000,000/Night Experience - Hour by Hour\n139\t\n140\t**Afternoon (after 16:30):**\n141\t1. Temple grounds close to public at 16:30\n142\t2. Guests welcomed through Niomon gate (exclusive access)\n143\t3. Private guided tour with a temple priest\n144\t4. Visit National Treasures and Important Cultural Properties normally closed to public\n145\t5. Cultural treasures curated specifically for each party's visit\n146\t\n147\t**Evening:**\n148\t6. Check-in to Shorin-an (prepared with seasonal flowers, selected furniture, cultural treasures chosen for each party)\n149\t7. Dinner in the Goten (palace area/former head priest's residence)\n150\t   - Choice: Shojin ryori Buddhist cuisine OR Kyoto-style kaiseki\n151\t   - Served on tatami at low tables\n152\t8. Entertainment options (guest's choice):\n153\t   - Geisha and maiko music/dance performance\n154\t   - Gagaku (courtly music) on traditional instruments\n155\t   - Noh theater performance\n156\t   - Ikebana demonstration\n157\t9. Full moon nights: Optional moon viewing with priestly chanting\n158\t10. Retire to Shorin-an\n159\t\n160\t**Morning:**\n161\t11. Join monks' daily morning prayer ritual in shinden hall\n162\t12. Dharma sermon\n163\t13. Garden meditation: Draw rock patterns seen at Goten gardens\n164\t14. Breakfast\n165\t\n166\t### Cultural Experiences Available\n167\t- Private tea ceremony in historic tea rooms\n168\t- Calligraphy workshops with skilled practitioners\n169\t- Private garden tour\n170\t- Temple staff pre-consult on food preferences and cultural interests\n171\t\n172\t### Maintenance Funding Gap\n173\t\n174\t- Temple collects admission fees from visitors to designated cultural assets\n175\t- **These assets represent fewer than 20% of structures**\n176\t- Admission revenue must cover maintenance of the **remaining 80% of facilities**\n177\t- In addition to structures, Ninnaji maintains vast land including the 88 sacred places of Omuro\n178\t- This chronic underfunding was the primary driver behind entering the luxury accommodation business\n179\t\n180\t### Media Coverage\n181\t- **Nippon.com**: \"The Million Yen Temple Experience\"\n182\t- **Robb Report**: \"You Can Now Spend the Night in One of Kyoto's Most Historic Temples\"\n183\t- **JNTO (Japan.travel)**: Featured as Heritage Stay and luxury experience\n184\t- **Wabunka-Lux**: Official booking partner\n185\t- Impact: Generated significant international press attention; positioned Ninnaji as a model for cultural heritage-funded tourism\n186\t\n187\t### Comparison with Koyasan Luxury\n188\t- Koyasan: Multiple luxury options (Eko-in Gachirin ~$700+/night), volume-based model across 51 temples\n189\t- Ninnaji: Single ultra-premium option (¥1M/night), exclusivity-based model at 1 UNESCO site\n190\t- Koyasan: Buddhist spiritual immersion focus; Ninnaji: Imperial/noble cultural immersion focus\n191\t\n192\t---\n193\t\n194\t## 3. Tsukiji Hongwanji Temple (Tokyo)\n195\t\n196\t### Cafe Tsumugi - 18-Dish Breakfast\n197\t\n198\t**Price**: ¥2,200 (tax included, current after renovation)\n199\t**Hours**: Breakfast 8:00-10:30 AM (limited quantities)\n200\t\n201\t**The 18 Dishes (seasonal variations):**\n202\t1. Rice porridge (okayu)\n203\t2. Miso soup (Jakomiso)\n204\t3. Tsukiji Shoro Honten's tamagoyaki (famous rolled omelette)\n205\t4. Tsukiji Edoichi's tsukudani (preserved seafood)\n206\t5. Duck with sansho pepper\n207\t6. Konnyaku shiroae (yam with tofu and sesame)\n208\t7. Tofu with yuzu and white bean paste\n209\t8. Matcha jelly\n210\t9. Umeboshi (Nanko pickled plum)\n211\t10. Yuba with ikura (salmon roe)\n212\t11. Tomato with dashi\n213\t12. Seasonal fruits\n214\t13. Grilled satoimo (Japanese taro) with miso paste\n215\t14. Tofu with sliced grapefruit\n216\t15. Seaweed pollack roe\n217\t16. Octopus salad with shio-koji\n218\t17. Deep-fried fish cake\n219\t18. Additional seasonal side dish\n220\t\n221\t**Why 18?**: Named after the **18th of Amitabha Buddha's 48 vows (Hongwan)**. In Jodo Shinshu Buddhism, the 18th vow is considered the most profound - the vow of universal salvation through faith. The number connects the breakfast to the temple's spiritual foundation.\n222\t\n223\t**Renovation**: November 2022, renovated as a \"book cafe\" with expanded seating and Buddhist/general reading library.\n224\t\n225\t### Restaurant Shisui (紫水)\n226\t\n227\t- **Location**: 1st Floor, Daiichi Dendo Kaikan (First Missionary Hall)\n228\t- **Hours**: 11:00 AM - 3:00 PM\n229\t- **Cuisine**: Traditional shojin ryori (recipes handed down through generations), gozen meals with Tsukiji-sourced fresh ingredients, monzen soba noodles\n230\t- **Seating types**: Table seats, tatami rooms, fully private rooms (4+ guests), large rooms (30-40 guests)\n231\t- **Pricing**: Mid-range Japanese dining\n232\t\n233\t### Official Shop\n234\t\n235\t- Temple bookstore with Buddhist texts and reference materials\n236\t- Religious paraphernalia (juzu beads, incense, etc.)\n237\t- Knickknacks and items bearing Tsukiji Hongwanji logo\n238\t- Located within the Information Center\n239\t\n240\t### Information Center & Facilities\n241\t\n242\t- **Opened**: 2017 (400th anniversary of Tsukiji Hongwanji)\n243\t- **Hours**: 8:00 AM - 9:00 PM year-round\n244\t- **Features**: Cafe Tsumugi, bookstore, 2nd-floor terrace with panoramic main hall view\n245\t\n246\t### Accommodation Facility\n247\t\n248\t- **Tsukiji Hongwanji Kaikan**: Houses wedding hall, lounge, Japanese restaurant, souvenir corner\n249\t- **Hotel**: Open to public, both Japanese-style and Western-style rooms at \"reasonable prices\"\n250\t- Specific room counts, pricing, and capacity not publicly detailed in available sources\n251\t\n252\t### Lunch Concert Program (Pipe Organ)\n253\t\n254\t- **Schedule**: Last Friday of every month\n255\t- **Time**: 12:20 PM start (~30 minutes)\n256\t- **Admission**: **Free**\n257\t- **Instrument**: 2,000-pipe organ designed by Walker (former West Germany manufacturer)\n258\t- **Installed**: Since 1970\n259\t- **Repertoire**: Buddhist hymns, baroque music, contemporary pieces\n260\t- **Format**: Some sessions combine Buddhist talk with performance\n261\t- **Special events**: Premium Gagaku Sutra and Pipe Organ Night Concert (special ticketed events)\n262\t\n263\t### \"Open Temple\" Rebrand History\n264\t\n265\t**Before (traditional temple model):**\n266\t- Closed-off appearance; large trees blocked view of main building from outside\n267\t- Primarily served existing parishioner community\n268\t- Limited engagement with general public\n269\t\n270\t**2015 Transformation:**\n271\t- **Yasunaga Yugen** appointed as head of Tsukiji Hongwanji\n272\t- Background: Former banker and business consultant turned Buddhist priest\n273\t- Key reforms:\n274\t  1. Cut down large trees blocking main building visibility from street\n275\t  2. Opened Information Center with cafe, shop, bookstore (2017)\n276\t  3. Created joint grave facility\n277\t  4. Added free visitor parking\n278\t  5. Launched lunchtime concert series\n279\t  6. Created \"open, inviting atmosphere\"\n280\t\n281\t**Impact:**\n282\t- Cafe Tsumugi became famous with constant waiting lines since 2017 opening\n283\t- Temple positioned as model for modern Buddhist engagement\n284\t- Significantly increased foot traffic (specific numbers not publicly available)\n285\t\n286\t### 2017 Transformation Details\n287\t\n288\t- **Architect (original building)**: Ito Chuta (伊藤忠太), University of Tokyo, built 1931-1934\n289\t- **Design concept**: Indian/Asian Buddhist architectural style (unique among Japanese temples)\n290\t- **2017 renovation budget**: Not publicly disclosed\n291\t- **Information Center design**: By Arflex Japan (furniture/interior)\n292\t\n293\t### Financial Model\n294\t\n295\tExact revenue breakdowns not publicly available, but revenue streams include:\n296\t1. Cafe Tsumugi (breakfast/lunch/cafe)\n297\t2. Restaurant Shisui\n298\t3. Kaikan hotel accommodation\n299\t4. Wedding hall\n300\t5. Shop/bookstore\n301\t6. Parishioner donations and ritual fees\n302\t7. Admission (free - offset by commercial revenues)\n303\t8. Events and concerts\n304\t\n305\t---\n306\t\n307\t## 4. D&DEPARTMENT KYOTO at Bukkoji Temple\n308\t\n309\t### Location & Setup\n310\t\n311\t- **Temple**: Bukkoji Temple (佛光寺), founded 1212 by Shinran Shonin (Jodo Shinshu founder), officially established by disciple Ryogen in 1324\n312\t- **Building**: Former tea house, over 150 years old, within temple precinct\n313\t- **Collaboration**: Three-way between Nagaoka Kenmei, Bukkoji Temple, and Kyoto University of Art and Design\n314\t\n315\t### Product Curation - 47 Prefectures Concept\n316\t\n317\t- **Core mission**: Identify and re-evaluate the uniqueness of Japan's 47 prefectures and their local designs\n318\t- **Goal**: Eventually have a shop in each of Japan's 47 prefectures\n319\t- **Product types**: Japanese sake sets, tea mugs, unique second-hand pieces, heirloom misos, specialty sesame seed oils, locally crafted goods\n320\t- **Selection criteria**: Items that demonstrate \"Long Life Design\" - universal design that transcends trends\n321\t\n322\t### d shokudo Menu & Pricing\n323\t\n324\t- **Signature**: \"Kyoto set meal\" - rice, soup, and Obanzai (Kyoto-style precooked dishes)\n325\t- **Menu rotation**: Monthly, reflecting seasonal Kyoto ingredients\n326\t- **Ingredients**: Kyoto-grown tea, local vegetables\n327\t- **Price range**: Mid-range (~¥1,000-2,000 for set meals based on review data)\n328\t- **Style**: Casual Japanese dining within temple setting\n329\t\n330\t### d cafe\n331\t\n332\t- Small-batch producer focus\n333\t- Rare items: heirloom misos, special sesame seed oils\n334\t- Adjacent to main shop area\n335\t- Japanese tea and seasonal beverages\n336\t\n337\t### Nagaoka Kenmei's \"Long Life Design\" Philosophy\n338\t\n339\t- **Definition**: Design whose life will last for a long time; universal design unaffected by trends\n340\t- **Approach**: Not about \"new\" but about \"enduring\" - curating products that have proven their worth over decades\n341\t- **Application**: Each D&Department store curates local products that embody this philosophy\n342\t- **Cultural mission**: Combat disposable consumer culture by celebrating durable, meaningful design\n343\t\n344\t### Rent/Lease Arrangement\n345\t\n346\t- Specific financial terms not publicly disclosed\n347\t- Operates within temple grounds as a cultural tenant\n348\t- Likely benefits from temple's tax-exempt land status\n349\t- Relationship is framed as cultural partnership rather than purely commercial lease\n350\t\n351\t### Annual Revenue & Visitors\n352\t\n353\t- Not publicly reported\n354\t- D&DEPARTMENT is a privately held company\n355\t- Operates stores across Japan (8+ locations) plus Seoul\n356\t\n357\t### Temple-Commercial Balance\n358\t\n359\t- Shop and restaurant operate within temple precinct but feel like separate cultural destination\n360\t- Temple maintains its religious functions independently\n361\t- The collaboration is positioned as \"sharing Kyoto culture\" rather than commercialization\n362\t- University partnership adds educational legitimacy\n363\t\n364\t### D&DEPARTMENT SEOUL Comparison\n365\t\n366\t- **Opened**: November 9, 2013 (8th store, first overseas location)\n367\t- **Location**: Itaewon area, Seoul\n368\t- **Partner**: MMMG (Millimeter Milligram)\n369\t- **Adaptation**: \"KOREA SELECT\" - discovers and introduces products unique to Korea\n370\t- **Products**: Traditional Korean crafts, specialty goods, local long-life products, recycled goods\n371\t- **Key difference**: Adapted 47 prefectures concept to Korean regional goods; same philosophy, localized execution\n372\t\n373\t---\n374\t\n375\t## 5. Temple Cafes (Tera Cafe) - Success and Failure\n376\t\n377\t### Tera Cafe Daikanyama (CLOSED)\n378\t\n379\t- **Status**: Confirmed closed (listed as [Closed] on Tabelog, official site shows 閉店のお知らせ)\n380\t- **Location**: Daikanyama, Shibuya-ku, Tokyo\n381\t- **Concept**: Temple cafe where monks were present; peaceful atmosphere\n382\t- **Offerings**:\n383\t  - Morning porridge specialty\n384\t  - Sutra copying experience\n385\t  - Buddhist lectures\n386\t  - \"Monk Bar\" (monthly) - chat with monks over alcohol (most popular event)\n387\t- **Closure reason**: Not publicly detailed; likely factors include high Daikanyama rent, disconnection from actual temple community, novelty-dependent business model\n388\t- **Timeline**: Opened ~2014, closed by early 2026\n389\t\n390\t### Tera Cafe Chaniwa (茶庭) at Manpukuji, Osaka (SURVIVING)\n391\t\n392\t- **Location**: Manpukuji Temple (萬福寺), Minami-Horie, central Osaka\n393\t- **Temple**: 400-year-old Jodo Shinshu Hongwanji temple\n394\t- **Opened**: April 2017\n395\t- **Operating model**: Only 3 days/week (Thursday, Friday, Saturday)\n396\t- **Features**: Garden terrace seating, original Japanese sweets\n397\t- **Menu**: Japanese sweets (wa-sweets), tea, seasonal items\n398\t- **Recognition**: SNS trending spot; visited by celebrities including Neymar and PSG players\n399\t- **Why it survives**:\n400\t  1. Located within actual temple grounds (no separate rent burden)\n401\t  2. Limited operating days reduce costs\n402\t  3. Integrated with temple events, music classes, workshops\n403\t  4. Functions as community hub, not just cafe\n404\t  5. Temple welcomes everyone - \"open community center\" philosophy\n405\t\n406\t### Key Differentiators: Success vs Failure\n407\t\n408\t| Factor | Failed (Daikanyama) | Survived (Chaniwa/Others) |\n409\t|--------|---------------------|---------------------------|\n410\t| Location | Standalone commercial area | Within actual temple grounds |\n411\t| Rent burden | High commercial rent | Temple property (low/no rent) |\n412\t| Operating days | Full week | Limited (3 days/week) |\n413\t| Community | Novelty-seeking visitors | Temple parishioners + neighbors |\n414\t| Revenue dependency | Cafe sales only | Multiple temple revenue streams |\n415\t| Authenticity | Monks brought in | Natural extension of temple life |\n416\t| Cost structure | High fixed costs | Low overhead |\n417\t\n418\t### Other Surviving Temple Cafe Examples\n419\t\n420\t- **Tsukiji Hongwanji Cafe Tsumugi**: Thriving since 2017 (see Section 3)\n421\t- **Various shukubo tea/cafe services**: Integrated into temple stay experiences\n422\t- **D&DEPARTMENT KYOTO d cafe**: Within Bukkoji Temple (see Section 4)\n423\t\n424\t### Average Revenue\n425\t\n426\tNot publicly reported for temple cafes specifically. The successful models tend to operate as cost centers that drive foot traffic and community engagement rather than standalone profit centers.\n427\t\n428\t---\n429\t\n430\t## 6. PlanetDAO\n431\t\n432\t### Investment Structure\n433\t\n434\t- **Minimum investment**: ¥34,540 (US$217)\n435\t- **Model**: Co-ownership through DAO (Decentralized Autonomous Organization)\n436\t- **Returns**:\n437\t  1. Capital gains when reselling shares\n438\t  2. Dividends from vacation rental business profits\n439\t  3. Some properties offer free accommodation for shareholders above certain share thresholds\n440\t- **Structure per property**: Each property held by a dedicated PlanetDAO company, supported by Planet Labs for structure and reporting\n441\t- **Operations**: Local operating partners manage daily operations\n442\t\n443\t### Ryogonji Temple (了源寺) - First Property\n444\t\n445\t- **Age**: 170-year-old temple\n446\t- **Location**: Irokawa district\n447\t- **Status**: Registered Tangible Cultural Property (since 2021)\n448\t- **How it started**: Local community introduced to PlanetDAO by Chief Monk seeking financial support\n449\t- **Investment round**: 1,000 shares distributed\n450\t- **Co-owners**: 134 investors from 15 countries\n451\t- **Operations partner**: ShareWing Co., Ltd. (managing lodging facilities)\n452\t\n453\t### Sado Island Property - The Hojo Heritage\n454\t\n455\t- **Age**: 350-year-old compound\n456\t- **Location**: Heart of Sado Island\n457\t- **Buildings**: 5 traditional structures (Important Cultural Properties / Registered Tangible Cultural Properties)\n458\t- **Architecture**: Blend of samurai, merchant, and rural styles spanning Edo and Meiji periods\n459\t- **History**: Hojo family practiced traditional medicine for 7 generations\n460\t- **Restoration estimate**: Main House alone: **¥600 million** (far beyond individual or even national support capacity)\n461\t\n462\t### Co-Owner Demographics\n463\t\n464\t- **Total countries represented**: 23 countries and counting (across all properties)\n465\t- **Ryogonji specifically**: 134 co-owners from 15 countries\n466\t- **Detailed nationality breakdown**: Not publicly disclosed\n467\t\n468\t### Legal Structure\n469\t\n470\t- **DAO framework**: Decentralized Autonomous Organization for governance/voting\n471\t- **Real estate compliance**: Spent nearly a year developing legal and business structure compliant with Japan's strict real estate and securities laws\n472\t- **Entity per property**: Each property held by dedicated company\n473\t- **Governance**: Co-owners participate in voting on key decisions\n474\t- **Founded**: 2023 by Tamaki Nishimura\n475\t\n476\t### Media Coverage & Partnerships\n477\t\n478\t- **South China Morning Post**: \"Abandoned Japanese temples are a great new tourism investment\"\n479\t- **NHK Ohayo Kansai**: Featured Hokoji Temple restoration (4th project)\n480\t- **Smart Money Asia**: Detailed feature on preservation model\n481\t- **Ikigai Tribe podcast**: Episode 93 featuring founder\n482\t- **POTLUCK AWARD 2025 finalist**: Organized by Mitsui Fudosan and NewsPicks (selected from 103 entries, top 12)\n483\t- **Nachi Project**: Latest expansion (Substack feature by founder)\n484\t\n485\t### Founder\n486\t- **Tamaki Nishimura**: Founded PlanetDAO 2023\n487\t- Background: Previous inbound tourism agency that didn't survive COVID-19; discovered DAO concept as solution\n488\t- Company: Planet Labs Inc.\n489\t\n490\t---\n491\t\n492\t## 7. Shinshoji Zen Museum & Gardens (Hiroshima)\n493\t\n494\t### Kohei Nawa x SANDWICH \"Kohtei\" Pavilion\n495\t\n496\t- **Completed**: 2016\n497\t- **Designers**: Kohei Nawa (artist), Yoshitaka Lee and Yuichi Kodai (architects) from SANDWICH, Nawa's Kyoto-based creative studio\n498\t- **Concept**: Ship-shaped building inspired by temple's founding to console spirits of those who died at sea (temple founded by shipbuilding company)\n499\t- **Materials**: Three fundamental materials - wood, stone, water\n500\t- **Exterior**: Kokerabuki (traditional wooden shingles), Japanese cypress cladding\n501\t- **Structure**: Raised above landscape on two rows of pillars, creating sheltered space beneath\n502\t- **Interior experience**: Meditation-like installation using water reflections and natural light\n503\t- **Description**: \"An architecture that floats on waves surrounded by mountains\"\n504\t\n505\t### Temple Grounds\n506\t\n507\t- **Total area**: 230,000 m² (70,000 tsubo)\n508\t- **Notable structures**:\n509\t  - 17th-century temple hall relocated from Shiga Prefecture\n510\t  - Reconstructed teahouse originally designed by Sen no Rikyu\n511\t  - Temple office designed by Terunobu Fujimori (theme: red pine)\n512\t\n513\t### Zen Painting Collection\n514\t\n515\t- Collection exists but specific notable pieces and exhibition rotation details not publicly detailed in available sources\n516\t\n517\t### Zazen Experience\n518\t\n519\t- **Individual zazen**: ¥1,000 per person, advance reservation required\n520\t- **Sutra copying**: ¥1,000 per person, no advance notice required\n521\t- **One-Day Zen Experience**: ¥5,600 per person, advance reservation required\n522\t  - Morning: Zen meditation\n523\t  - Lunch: Yudame udon (warm udon with dipping sauce, served in Rinzai training hall style)\n524\t  - Afternoon: Sutra copying\n525\t  - Closing: Tea at one of the teahouses\n526\t\n527\t### Entry Fee\n528\t\n529\t- Adults: ¥1,800\n530\t- University/high school students: ¥1,000\n531\t- Junior high/elementary: ¥500\n532\t\n533\t### Workation Plan\n534\t\n535\t- Listed in navigation menu of official site, indicating the program exists\n536\t- Specific details (pricing, duration, amenities) not publicly detailed\n537\t\n538\t### Hiroshima Architecture Exhibition 2025\n539\t\n540\t- **Event**: Inaugural Hiroshima Architecture Exhibition 2025\n541\t- **Dates**: October 4 - November 30, 2025\n542\t- **Format**: Triennial event\n543\t- **Organizer**: Kambara & Tsuneishi Foundation\n544\t- **Shinshoji's role**: Hosted \"Next Architecture: Connecting Futures Through Design\"\n545\t- **Exhibitors at Shinshoji**: Sou Fujimoto, Junya Ishigami, Norihisa Kawashima, Hiroki Akiyoshi (VUILD), Clouds Architecture Office\n546\t- **Special feature**: Preview exhibition of Kenzo Tange's self-designed 1953 Tokyo residence (planned reconstruction in Fukuyama), including 1/3-scale model\n547\t- **Scope**: 23 architects and artists from Japan and abroad across Fukuyama and Onomichi\n548\t\n549\t### Annual Visitor Numbers\n550\t\n551\t- Not publicly reported. Described as attracting \"many Japanese interested in Zazen\" and a smaller percentage of foreign visitors\n552\t\n553\t---\n554\t\n555\t## 8. teamLab Temple Collaborations\n556\t\n557\t### Toji (東寺) Project - Kyoto\n558\t\n559\t- **Exhibition**: \"teamLab: Digitized World Heritage Site of Toji\"\n560\t- **Dates**: August 6 - September 19, 2021\n561\t- **Temple**: World Heritage Site, built 796\n562\t- **Pricing**:\n563\t  - Adults: ¥1,600 (weekdays) / ¥2,200 (weekends/holidays)\n564\t  - Students: ¥600 (weekdays) / ¥800 (weekends/holidays)\n565\t  - Preschoolers: Free\n566\t- **Key installations**:\n567\t  - Floating Resonating Lamps on gourd pond (autonomous, sound-responsive)\n568\t  - Kodo flower cycle installation (interactive - flowers scatter when touched)\n569\t- **Concept**: \"Digitized City\" - non-material digital technology turns buildings into art without physical alteration\n570\t- **Visitor numbers**: Not specifically reported; teamLab Biovortex Kyoto (permanent museum) reached 500,000 visitors by February 2026\n571\t\n572\t### Engyoji (圓教寺) Project - Himeji\n573\t\n574\t- **Exhibition**: \"teamLab: Existence in Perception - Engyoji Temple\"\n575\t- **Date**: Launched May 2023\n576\t- **Location**: Jiki-do hall (Important Cultural Property, Muromachi period 1336-1573)\n577\t- **Space**: 38m-long first floor hall transformed into artwork space\n578\t- **Key works**:\n579\t  - \"Massless Sun, Distorted Space\" - floating sphere of light that changes position as viewer moves\n580\t  - \"Giant Solidified Spark\" - new installation\n581\t- **Concept**: Questions the notion of existence\n582\t- **Building**: Originally multi-purpose space where monks studied, slept, and ate\n583\t\n584\t### Kodaiji (高台寺) Projection Mapping - Kyoto\n585\t\n586\t- **History**: Seasonal illuminations 3 times per year (spring, summer, autumn)\n587\t- **Temple**: Rinzai Zen temple, founded 1606 by Nene (widow of Hideyoshi Toyotomi)\n588\t- **Technology**: Computer-generated 3D images projected onto architecture and landscapes\n589\t- **Location**: Hojo (main hall), projected onto Hashintei karesansui (raked-sand garden) and gate\n590\t- **Duration**: 3-minute dynamic show combining light, sound, historic architecture\n591\t\n592\t**2026 Spring Schedule:**\n593\t- Dates: March 13 - May 6, 2026\n594\t- Hours: 17:00-21:30 (gates close 22:00)\n595\t- Admission: ¥800 (Kodaiji) / ¥500 (adjacent Entoku-in)\n596\t- Theme: \"Eternal Bonds Spring - Nene and Matsu\" (friendship between two historical women)\n597\t\n598\t### Zuigan-ji x NAKED - Matsushima\n599\t\n600\t- **Event**: \"NAKED Matsushima - National Treasure Zuiganji Temple\"\n601\t- **Note**: This is a **NAKED Inc.** project, not teamLab\n602\t- **2025 dates**: October 24 - November 24, 2025\n603\t- **Hours**: 17:00-21:00 (last admission 20:45)\n604\t- **Theme**: \"Moon Viewing Light-Up\" - the moon that Date Masamune cherished\n605\t- **Features**:\n606\t  - Projection mapping in main hall front garden and kitchen (beams, arabesque carvings)\n607\t  - NAKED Distance Lantern (participatory art)\n608\t  - Special access to rooms normally off-limits\n609\t- **Temple**: National Treasure, erected 1609 by Date Masamune\n610\t- **Booking**: Available on Klook\n611\t\n612\t### Revenue Model\n613\t\n614\t- **General model**: teamLab charges admission, likely revenue-sharing with venue\n615\t- **Specific splits**: Not publicly disclosed\n616\t- **teamLab's approach**: Insists on ticketing model (proven since 2017 Silicon Valley show)\n617\t- **Impact on regular visitors**: Temple installations typically run during off-hours or specific seasons, complementing rather than replacing regular visitation\n618\t\n619\t---\n620\t\n621\t## 9. TERA WORK / ShareWing\n622\t\n623\t### Company Overview\n624\t\n625\t- **Full name**: ShareWing Co., Ltd.\n626\t- **Founded**: 2016\n627\t- **Co-CEOs**: Naoko Unrinin and Koji Ueda\n628\t- **Brand**: OTERA STAY\n629\t- **Expansion**: Also launched \"JINJA STAY\" (shrine stays) - first at Mt. Oyama/Afuri Shrine, Isehara City\n630\t\n631\t### Number of Participating Temples\n632\t\n633\t- As of 2022: 11 temples\n634\t- Growing to 13+ temples nationwide (recent data)\n635\t- Over 20 affiliated temples mentioned in broader network\n636\t\n637\t### TERA WORK (Telework) Pricing Model\n638\t\n639\t**Initial launch locations (5 temples):**\n640\t1. Shodenji Temple - Tokyo\n641\t2. Zenkoji Temple - Gifu Prefecture (Takayama)\n642\t3. Kanon-in Temple - Gunma Prefecture (Kiryu)\n643\t4. Habanobo Shukubo - Yamanashi Prefecture (Minamikoma)\n644\t5. Daitaiji Temple - Wakayama Prefecture (Shimowada)\n645\t\n646\t- **Concept**: \"Tera\" (temple) + \"tele\" (telework) = TERA WORK\n647\t- **Model**: Temple grounds as shared workspace, combining inn services with Buddhist experiences\n648\t- **Experiences included**: Zazen meditation, sutra copying, Buddhist teaching lectures\n649\t\n650\t### Temple Camp / RV Park\n651\t\n652\t- **Location**: Temple Hotel Daitaiji (Nachikatsuura, Wakayama) along Kumano Kodo\n653\t- **Facilities**: Recreational Vehicle parks with power sources\n654\t- **Options**: Camper-van stays, car camping sites\n655\t- **Setting**: \"Pure air of Kumano Kodo\"\n656\t\n657\t### Cloud Hotel / Online Experience\n658\t\n659\t- Limited public information available\n660\t- ShareWing focuses primarily on physical temple experiences\n661\t- Recent launch: Well-being tourism program with Kouzakiji Temple (March 2025)\n662\t\n663\t### Corporate Training Packages\n664\t\n665\t- **Custom**: \"OTERA WORK\" service for corporate use (training camps, new employee training)\n666\t- **Capacity**: Suitable for small groups to corporate retreats of 100+ people\n667\t- **Example**: Hokoji Temple - limited to 1 group/day, max 70 people\n668\t- **Pricing**: From ~¥100,000/person (varies by season and program)\n669\t- **Process**: Concierge-style; ShareWing interviews guests about preferences, designs custom plans considering temple capabilities and client budget\n670\t- **Digital nomad**: \"Digital Nomad Retreat\" program at temples along UNESCO Kumano Kodo\n671\t\n672\t---\n673\t\n674\t## 10. Temple Events / Community\n675\t\n676\t### World Heritage Temple Yoga Events\n677\t\n678\t- **Operator**: OTERA STAY (ShareWing) offers Zen Temple Yoga Retreats\n679\t- **Setting**: Temples with deep history and tradition\n680\t- **Format**: Yoga combined with Buddhist meditation practices\n681\t- **Booking**: Through OTERA STAY platform and partner sites\n682\t- **Specific pricing**: Varies by temple and program\n683\t\n684\t### Temple Matchmaking (寺コン / Tera-Kon)\n685\t\n686\t**How it works:**\n687\t1. Singles are interviewed by temple staff\n688\t2. Signed up to national network of matching agencies\n689\t3. Temple arranges meetings with other singles\n690\t4. Buddhist approach: emphasis on willingness to adapt to each other (not just shared values)\n691\t\n692\t**Key example - Tsukiji Hongwanji:**\n693\t- Service: \"Tsukiji no Tera-Kon\" (築地の寺コン)\n694\t- Launch: July (received 100+ inquiries since launch)\n695\t- Philosophy: Monk Koshi Kimiya encourages \"open mind\" approach to marriage\n696\t\n697\t**Demographics:**\n698\t- Male members: concentrated in 30s-40s, few above 50\n699\t- Female members: span 20s-70s but concentrated in 30s-40s\n700\t- Overall: more female than male members\n701\t- Appeal factor: \"People feel comfortable turning to us because we are working for a temple\"\n702\t\n703\t**Context**: Japan's marriage rate dropped from ~10% (early 1970s) to 4.8% (2019)\n704\t\n705\t### Rakugo Performances in Temples\n706\t\n707\t**Example - Gangoji Temple (World Heritage):**\n708\t- **Event**: \"World Heritage Night: Gangoji Yose\"\n709\t- **Location**: Zen Room (usually non-public space)\n710\t- **Content**: Professional rakugo and kodan performances\n711\t- **Bonus**: Exclusive access to Gokurakudo Hall of Gangoji Temple for participants\n712\t- **Format**: Traditional storytelling in historic Buddhist setting\n713\t\n714\t**Broader trend:**\n715\t- English Rakugo Project launched (Tokyo Heritage Partners)\n716\t- Performers like Ryusuitei Hanabi promoted internationally\n717\t- Pricing: Typically ¥2,000-5,000 per performance (varies by venue)\n718\t\n719\t### Seasonal Events - Specific Examples\n720\t\n721\t1. **Kodaiji Illuminations**: 3x/year (spring, summer, autumn), ¥800 admission\n722\t2. **NAKED Zuiganji**: Annual autumn event (Oct-Nov), nighttime access\n723\t3. **Koyasan Okunoin Night Tour**: Year-round, ¥5,000\n724\t4. **teamLab Toji**: Limited seasonal runs, ¥1,600-2,200\n725\t5. **Ninnaji Moon Viewing**: Full moon nights, included in ¥1M stay\n726\t6. **Tsukiji Hongwanji Pipe Organ Concert**: Monthly (last Friday), free\n727\t7. **Shinshoji One-Day Zen**: Year-round by reservation, ¥5,600\n728\t\n729\t---\n730\t\n731\t## Cross-Cutting Insights\n732\t\n733\t### Revenue Model Spectrum\n734\t\n735\t| Model | Example | Price Point | Volume |\n736\t|-------|---------|-------------|--------|\n737\t| Ultra-luxury exclusive | Ninnaji | ¥1,000,000/night | 1 party/night |\n738\t| Premium accommodation | Eko-in Gachirin | ~¥100,000+/night | Low |\n739\t| Standard shukubo | Koyasan 51 temples | ¥15,000-50,000/night | High |\n740\t| F&B + retail | Tsukiji Hongwanji | ¥2,200 breakfast | Very high |\n741\t| Design retail | D&DEPARTMENT | ¥1,000-2,000 meals | Medium |\n742\t| Micro-investment | PlanetDAO | ¥34,540 minimum | 134+ co-owners |\n743\t| Art/event ticketing | teamLab/NAKED | ¥800-2,200 | High volume |\n744\t| Corporate retreat | OTERA STAY | ¥100,000+/person | Group |\n745\t\n746\t### Success Factors\n747\t\n748\t1. **Authentic integration**: Most successful models embed commercial activity within genuine temple practice\n749\t2. **Low overhead**: Using temple property eliminates major cost burden\n750\t3. **Multiple revenue streams**: Temples that diversify (F&B + accommodation + events + retail) are more resilient\n751\t4. **Story and meaning**: Every successful case has a compelling narrative (Amitabha's 18th vow, Kobo Daishi, Long Life Design)\n752\t5. **Seasonal programming**: Adapting offerings to seasonal cycles (cherry blossom, autumn foliage, New Year)\n753\t6. **International appeal**: Foreign visitor segment is growing and willing to pay premium for authentic experiences\n754\t\n755\t---\n756\t\n757\t*Sources compiled from web research across official sites, travel platforms, news outlets, and specialist publications. See inline references for specific data origins.*\n758\t"}]},"uuid":"6bfb8b23-2208-48ac-834a-a3d01342fd71","timestamp":"2026-07-29T00:13:35.186Z","toolUseResult":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/Users/choi_ai/do-better-workspace/10-projects/15-bongeunsa/39-japanese-temple-business-models-research.md","content":"# Japanese Temple/Shrine Business Models - Detailed Research\n\n> Research Date: 2026-05-19\n> Purpose: 봉은사 구 웨딩홀 리뉴얼 프로젝트 참고자료\n\n---\n\n## 1. Koyasan Shukubo Network (Mt. Koya, Wakayama)\n\n### Top-Rated Shukubo Temples\n\n**Eko-in (恵光院)**\n- Koyasan's most popular temple lodging, famous for Goma fire ritual\n- Room types:\n  - **Gachirin (月輪) Suite**: 100m², largest suite in all Koyasan shukubo. Opened March 2022. Features spacious bedroom, private semi-open-air bath, garden view. Price: ~$712/night average (luxury tier, exact JPY varies by season; peak autumn can exceed ¥50,000+ for standard rooms)\n  - **Kongo Jr. Suite**: 60m², opened April 2024. Japanese-style room + bedroom + veranda\n  - **Tenjiku Jr. Suite**: 65m², living room + Japanese room + bedroom + 15m² private garden\n  - **Hisui Deluxe Room**: 50m², bedroom + Japanese room, panoramic town view, private bath\n  - Standard rooms: tatami + futon, shared facilities. From ~$215/night\n- Experiences: Morning religious service, Goma fire ritual, ajikan meditation, sutra copying\n- Public baths: 16:00-22:00 and 06:00-09:00\n\n**Fukuchi-in (福智院)**\n- Distinguished by having its own natural hot spring onsen (rare among shukubo)\n- Traditional Japanese garden designed by Shigemori Mirei (重森三玲), one of Japan's most famous garden designers\n- Shojin ryori meals included in stay\n- Japanese-style rooms with garden views\n\n**Shojoshin-in (清浄心院)**\n- Founded in Tencho era (822-83), one of the oldest temples on Mt. Koya (3 years older than Kongobuji)\n- Prime location: right at the entrance to Okunoin path\n- Features: \"Hanare\" private guest residence with private bath, Japanese gardens, pond\n- Fire ceremony daily at 13:00\n- Morning service followed by temple treasures tour (in Japanese)\n- Guests describe dinner as \"10+ vegetarian dishes, absolutely fantastic\"\n\n### Shojin Ryori (Temple Cuisine) Details\n\n**Course Structure:**\n- Deluxe: 3 courses, ~12 items\n- Standard: 2 courses, ~10 items\n- Simple: 2 courses, ~9 items\n\n**Five Principles:**\n- Five methods: boiling, baking, steaming, frying, raw\n- Five colors: red, yellow, green, white, black\n- Five flavors: sweet, sour, spicy, bitter, salty\n\n**Signature Dishes:**\n- **Goma Tofu (胡麻豆腐)**: Most iconic. Made with kuzuko (arrowroot powder) + ground sesame (not soy)\n- **Shojin Hanazukushi**: Served in flower basket; shiitake shaped like mackerel, persimmon-leaf wrapped sushi, Koya vegetarian meatloaf\n- Typical dinner spread: tofu varieties, tempura, hot pot, fresh-cut vegetables, mountain plants, pickled vegetables, soups, rice\n- All strictly vegan: no meat, fish, eggs, or pungent alliums (garlic, onion)\n\n**Lunch Experience (Eko-in):**\n- Available to non-staying visitors via \"More Koyasan\" booking\n- Shojin ryori lunch experience bookable through byfood.com\n\n### Morning Prayer Ceremony\n\n- **Eko-in schedule**: Religious service at 7:00 AM daily in main hall, followed by Goma (Homa) Fire Ritual at 7:30 AM at the Bishamondo\n- **General pattern**: Most shukubo hold morning prayers 6:00-7:00 AM\n- **Duration**: ~30-45 minutes for service, additional 15-20 minutes for fire ritual\n- **What happens**: Sutra chanting, incense offering, fire ritual (at temples offering Goma). Guests may participate or observe\n- Not all temples perform fire rituals; Eko-in is one of the few that does\n\n### Okunoin Night Cemetery Tour\n\n- **Operator**: Awesome Tours (official licensed tour)\n- **Check-in**: 13:00-17:00 and 18:30-19:00\n- **Departure**: 19:00-19:15\n- **End time**: 20:20 at Okunoin Gokusho\n- **Duration**: ~90 minutes walking tour\n- **Price**: ¥5,000 per person (from March 11, 2025; previously ¥4,500)\n- **Language**: Always 2 English-speaking guides available\n- **Content**: Walk through Japan's largest historical cemetery, stories of Kobo Daishi, Shingon Esoteric Buddhism teachings, cemetery legends, monk daily life, Koyasan history, notable tombs\n- **Booking**: Separate from shukubo stay; advance reservation required\n- **Atmosphere**: 200,000+ tombstones, ancient cedar trees, stone lanterns, described as \"unforgettable\" and \"atmospheric\"\n\n### Foreign Visitor Demographics\n\n**Japan-wide context (2024-2025):**\n- Record 42.7M international visitors to Japan in 2025 (+15.8% YoY)\n- Total spending: ~USD 60B (36.6% hotels, 27% shopping, 21.9% F&B)\n- Top source markets: South Korea (9.5M), China (9.1M), Taiwan (6.8M)\n- 35% solo travelers in 2024, 97% of solo travelers aged 18-28\n- 70% of visitors stay in 3 major metro areas; 30% visit regional destinations\n\n**Koyasan-specific patterns:**\n- Strong Western European and Australian visitor base (historically popular with backpackers since 1990s)\n- Growing Asian visitor segment post-COVID\n- Tour groups from France, UK, Australia particularly prominent\n- Specific Koyasan demographics not publicly reported\n\n### Seasonal Occupancy Patterns\n\n- **Cherry Blossom**: Late April to early May (Koyasan blooms later than lowlands)\n- **Summer Peak**: Late July-August (school groups flood Koyasan)\n- **Autumn Foliage**: Peak around first week of November - **highest demand period**\n  - Prices surge significantly; standard rooms can exceed ¥50,000/night\n  - Must book 6-12 months in advance for preferred temples\n- **Winter**: Lowest occupancy, snow-covered landscape, unique atmosphere\n- **New Year**: Brief spike for Hatsumode visitors\n\n### Koyasan Shukubo Association (1958)\n\n- **Legal entity**: Corporation established 1958\n- **Members**: 51 shukubo (temple stay facilities)\n- **Registration**: Licensed travel agency in Wakayama Prefecture\n- **Operations**:\n  - 3 tourist information centers: Central, Ichinohashi, Nakanohashi\n  - Official online booking system (koyasan-shukubo.net)\n  - \"More Koyasan\" pre-bookable experience platform\n  - Souvenir/book sales, cafe operations\n  - Electric bicycle and GPS guide device rentals\n- **Marketing**: Operates bilingual (JP/EN) websites, partnerships with OTAs (Booking.com, Agoda, Jalan, etc.)\n\n---\n\n## 2. Ninnaji Temple Luxury Stay (Kyoto)\n\n### \"Iroha Nihon\" Program\n\n- **Organizer**: Nippon Foundation (日本財団)\n- **Mission**: Communicate \"the soul of Japan\" through cultural experiences at historic sites\n- **Launch at Ninnaji**: Spring 2018\n- **Structure**: Foundation subsidizes renovation; temple operates accommodation; revenue partially funds cultural preservation\n\n### Accommodation - Shorin-an (松林庵)\n\n- **Building history**: Former residence of Hisatomi family (samurai/physicians attached to Ninnaji)\n- **How it came to be**: Donated structure, moved to temple grounds, fell into disrepair. Young temple staff created a plan and applied to Iroha Nihon program\n- **Renovation cost**: ¥157 million total; **80% covered by Nippon Foundation subsidy**\n- **Design**: Mix of traditional and contemporary Japanese styles\n- **Features**: Traditional tearoom, private gardens, raised futon beds, lofty duvets, locally crafted furnishings, modern bathroom, A/C, Wi-Fi\n- **Capacity**: Maximum 5 guests per night, 1 party only\n\n### The ¥1,000,000/Night Experience - Hour by Hour\n\n**Afternoon (after 16:30):**\n1. Temple grounds close to public at 16:30\n2. Guests welcomed through Niomon gate (exclusive access)\n3. Private guided tour with a temple priest\n4. Visit National Treasures and Important Cultural Properties normally closed to public\n5. Cultural treasures curated specifically for each party's visit\n\n**Evening:**\n6. Check-in to Shorin-an (prepared with seasonal flowers, selected furniture, cultural treasures chosen for each party)\n7. Dinner in the Goten (palace area/former head priest's residence)\n   - Choice: Shojin ryori Buddhist cuisine OR Kyoto-style kaiseki\n   - Served on tatami at low tables\n8. Entertainment options (guest's choice):\n   - Geisha and maiko music/dance performance\n   - Gagaku (courtly music) on traditional instruments\n   - Noh theater performance\n   - Ikebana demonstration\n9. Full moon nights: Optional moon viewing with priestly chanting\n10. Retire to Shorin-an\n\n**Morning:**\n11. Join monks' daily morning prayer ritual in shinden hall\n12. Dharma sermon\n13. Garden meditation: Draw rock patterns seen at Goten gardens\n14. Breakfast\n\n### Cultural Experiences Available\n- Private tea ceremony in historic tea rooms\n- Calligraphy workshops with skilled practitioners\n- Private garden tour\n- Temple staff pre-consult on food preferences and cultural interests\n\n### Maintenance Funding Gap\n\n- Temple collects admission fees from visitors to designated cultural assets\n- **These assets represent fewer than 20% of structures**\n- Admission revenue must cover maintenance of the **remaining 80% of facilities**\n- In addition to structures, Ninnaji maintains vast land including the 88 sacred places of Omuro\n- This chronic underfunding was the primary driver behind entering the luxury accommodation business\n\n### Media Coverage\n- **Nippon.com**: \"The Million Yen Temple Experience\"\n- **Robb Report**: \"You Can Now Spend the Night in One of Kyoto's Most Historic Temples\"\n- **JNTO (Japan.travel)**: Featured as Heritage Stay and luxury experience\n- **Wabunka-Lux**: Official booking partner\n- Impact: Generated significant international press attention; positioned Ninnaji as a model for cultural heritage-funded tourism\n\n### Comparison with Koyasan Luxury\n- Koyasan: Multiple luxury options (Eko-in Gachirin ~$700+/night), volume-based model across 51 temples\n- Ninnaji: Single ultra-premium option (¥1M/night), exclusivity-based model at 1 UNESCO site\n- Koyasan: Buddhist spiritual immersion focus; Ninnaji: Imperial/noble cultural immersion focus\n\n---\n\n## 3. Tsukiji Hongwanji Temple (Tokyo)\n\n### Cafe Tsumugi - 18-Dish Breakfast\n\n**Price**: ¥2,200 (tax included, current after renovation)\n**Hours**: Breakfast 8:00-10:30 AM (limited quantities)\n\n**The 18 Dishes (seasonal variations):**\n1. Rice porridge (okayu)\n2. Miso soup (Jakomiso)\n3. Tsukiji Shoro Honten's tamagoyaki (famous rolled omelette)\n4. Tsukiji Edoichi's tsukudani (preserved seafood)\n5. Duck with sansho pepper\n6. Konnyaku shiroae (yam with tofu and sesame)\n7. Tofu with yuzu and white bean paste\n8. Matcha jelly\n9. Umeboshi (Nanko pickled plum)\n10. Yuba with ikura (salmon roe)\n11. Tomato with dashi\n12. Seasonal fruits\n13. Grilled satoimo (Japanese taro) with miso paste\n14. Tofu with sliced grapefruit\n15. Seaweed pollack roe\n16. Octopus salad with shio-koji\n17. Deep-fried fish cake\n18. Additional seasonal side dish\n\n**Why 18?**: Named after the **18th of Amitabha Buddha's 48 vows (Hongwan)**. In Jodo Shinshu Buddhism, the 18th vow is considered the most profound - the vow of universal salvation through faith. The number connects the breakfast to the temple's spiritual foundation.\n\n**Renovation**: November 2022, renovated as a \"book cafe\" with expanded seating and Buddhist/general reading library.\n\n### Restaurant Shisui (紫水)\n\n- **Location**: 1st Floor, Daiichi Dendo Kaikan (First Missionary Hall)\n- **Hours**: 11:00 AM - 3:00 PM\n- **Cuisine**: Traditional shojin ryori (recipes handed down through generations), gozen meals with Tsukiji-sourced fresh ingredients, monzen soba noodles\n- **Seating types**: Table seats, tatami rooms, fully private rooms (4+ guests), large rooms (30-40 guests)\n- **Pricing**: Mid-range Japanese dining\n\n### Official Shop\n\n- Temple bookstore with Buddhist texts and reference materials\n- Religious paraphernalia (juzu beads, incense, etc.)\n- Knickknacks and items bearing Tsukiji Hongwanji logo\n- Located within the Information Center\n\n### Information Center & Facilities\n\n- **Opened**: 2017 (400th anniversary of Tsukiji Hongwanji)\n- **Hours**: 8:00 AM - 9:00 PM year-round\n- **Features**: Cafe Tsumugi, bookstore, 2nd-floor terrace with panoramic main hall view\n\n### Accommodation Facility\n\n- **Tsukiji Hongwanji Kaikan**: Houses wedding hall, lounge, Japanese restaurant, souvenir corner\n- **Hotel**: Open to public, both Japanese-style and Western-style rooms at \"reasonable prices\"\n- Specific room counts, pricing, and capacity not publicly detailed in available sources\n\n### Lunch Concert Program (Pipe Organ)\n\n- **Schedule**: Last Friday of every month\n- **Time**: 12:20 PM start (~30 minutes)\n- **Admission**: **Free**\n- **Instrument**: 2,000-pipe organ designed by Walker (former West Germany manufacturer)\n- **Installed**: Since 1970\n- **Repertoire**: Buddhist hymns, baroque music, contemporary pieces\n- **Format**: Some sessions combine Buddhist talk with performance\n- **Special events**: Premium Gagaku Sutra and Pipe Organ Night Concert (special ticketed events)\n\n### \"Open Temple\" Rebrand History\n\n**Before (traditional temple model):**\n- Closed-off appearance; large trees blocked view of main building from outside\n- Primarily served existing parishioner community\n- Limited engagement with general public\n\n**2015 Transformation:**\n- **Yasunaga Yugen** appointed as head of Tsukiji Hongwanji\n- Background: Former banker and business consultant turned Buddhist priest\n- Key reforms:\n  1. Cut down large trees blocking main building visibility from street\n  2. Opened Information Center with cafe, shop, bookstore (2017)\n  3. Created joint grave facility\n  4. Added free visitor parking\n  5. Launched lunchtime concert series\n  6. Created \"open, inviting atmosphere\"\n\n**Impact:**\n- Cafe Tsumugi became famous with constant waiting lines since 2017 opening\n- Temple positioned as model for modern Buddhist engagement\n- Significantly increased foot traffic (specific numbers not publicly available)\n\n### 2017 Transformation Details\n\n- **Architect (original building)**: Ito Chuta (伊藤忠太), University of Tokyo, built 1931-1934\n- **Design concept**: Indian/Asian Buddhist architectural style (unique among Japanese temples)\n- **2017 renovation budget**: Not publicly disclosed\n- **Information Center design**: By Arflex Japan (furniture/interior)\n\n### Financial Model\n\nExact revenue breakdowns not publicly available, but revenue streams include:\n1. Cafe Tsumugi (breakfast/lunch/cafe)\n2. Restaurant Shisui\n3. Kaikan hotel accommodation\n4. Wedding hall\n5. Shop/bookstore\n6. Parishioner donations and ritual fees\n7. Admission (free - offset by commercial revenues)\n8. Events and concerts\n\n---\n\n## 4. D&DEPARTMENT KYOTO at Bukkoji Temple\n\n### Location & Setup\n\n- **Temple**: Bukkoji Temple (佛光寺), founded 1212 by Shinran Shonin (Jodo Shinshu founder), officially established by disciple Ryogen in 1324\n- **Building**: Former tea house, over 150 years old, within temple precinct\n- **Collaboration**: Three-way between Nagaoka Kenmei, Bukkoji Temple, and Kyoto University of Art and Design\n\n### Product Curation - 47 Prefectures Concept\n\n- **Core mission**: Identify and re-evaluate the uniqueness of Japan's 47 prefectures and their local designs\n- **Goal**: Eventually have a shop in each of Japan's 47 prefectures\n- **Product types**: Japanese sake sets, tea mugs, unique second-hand pieces, heirloom misos, specialty sesame seed oils, locally crafted goods\n- **Selection criteria**: Items that demonstrate \"Long Life Design\" - universal design that transcends trends\n\n### d shokudo Menu & Pricing\n\n- **Signature**: \"Kyoto set meal\" - rice, soup, and Obanzai (Kyoto-style precooked dishes)\n- **Menu rotation**: Monthly, reflecting seasonal Kyoto ingredients\n- **Ingredients**: Kyoto-grown tea, local vegetables\n- **Price range**: Mid-range (~¥1,000-2,000 for set meals based on review data)\n- **Style**: Casual Japanese dining within temple setting\n\n### d cafe\n\n- Small-batch producer focus\n- Rare items: heirloom misos, special sesame seed oils\n- Adjacent to main shop area\n- Japanese tea and seasonal beverages\n\n### Nagaoka Kenmei's \"Long Life Design\" Philosophy\n\n- **Definition**: Design whose life will last for a long time; universal design unaffected by trends\n- **Approach**: Not about \"new\" but about \"enduring\" - curating products that have proven their worth over decades\n- **Application**: Each D&Department store curates local products that embody this philosophy\n- **Cultural mission**: Combat disposable consumer culture by celebrating durable, meaningful design\n\n### Rent/Lease Arrangement\n\n- Specific financial terms not publicly disclosed\n- Operates within temple grounds as a cultural tenant\n- Likely benefits from temple's tax-exempt land status\n- Relationship is framed as cultural partnership rather than purely commercial lease\n\n### Annual Revenue & Visitors\n\n- Not publicly reported\n- D&DEPARTMENT is a privately held company\n- Operates stores across Japan (8+ locations) plus Seoul\n\n### Temple-Commercial Balance\n\n- Shop and restaurant operate within temple precinct but feel like separate cultural destination\n- Temple maintains its religious functions independently\n- The collaboration is positioned as \"sharing Kyoto culture\" rather than commercialization\n- University partnership adds educational legitimacy\n\n### D&DEPARTMENT SEOUL Comparison\n\n- **Opened**: November 9, 2013 (8th store, first overseas location)\n- **Location**: Itaewon area, Seoul\n- **Partner**: MMMG (Millimeter Milligram)\n- **Adaptation**: \"KOREA SELECT\" - discovers and introduces products unique to Korea\n- **Products**: Traditional Korean crafts, specialty goods, local long-life products, recycled goods\n- **Key difference**: Adapted 47 prefectures concept to Korean regional goods; same philosophy, localized execution\n\n---\n\n## 5. Temple Cafes (Tera Cafe) - Success and Failure\n\n### Tera Cafe Daikanyama (CLOSED)\n\n- **Status**: Confirmed closed (listed as [Closed] on Tabelog, official site shows 閉店のお知らせ)\n- **Location**: Daikanyama, Shibuya-ku, Tokyo\n- **Concept**: Temple cafe where monks were present; peaceful atmosphere\n- **Offerings**:\n  - Morning porridge specialty\n  - Sutra copying experience\n  - Buddhist lectures\n  - \"Monk Bar\" (monthly) - chat with monks over alcohol (most popular event)\n- **Closure reason**: Not publicly detailed; likely factors include high Daikanyama rent, disconnection from actual temple community, novelty-dependent business model\n- **Timeline**: Opened ~2014, closed by early 2026\n\n### Tera Cafe Chaniwa (茶庭) at Manpukuji, Osaka (SURVIVING)\n\n- **Location**: Manpukuji Temple (萬福寺), Minami-Horie, central Osaka\n- **Temple**: 400-year-old Jodo Shinshu Hongwanji temple\n- **Opened**: April 2017\n- **Operating model**: Only 3 days/week (Thursday, Friday, Saturday)\n- **Features**: Garden terrace seating, original Japanese sweets\n- **Menu**: Japanese sweets (wa-sweets), tea, seasonal items\n- **Recognition**: SNS trending spot; visited by celebrities including Neymar and PSG players\n- **Why it survives**:\n  1. Located within actual temple grounds (no separate rent burden)\n  2. Limited operating days reduce costs\n  3. Integrated with temple events, music classes, workshops\n  4. Functions as community hub, not just cafe\n  5. Temple welcomes everyone - \"open community center\" philosophy\n\n### Key Differentiators: Success vs Failure\n\n| Factor | Failed (Daikanyama) | Survived (Chaniwa/Others) |\n|--------|---------------------|---------------------------|\n| Location | Standalone commercial area | Within actual temple grounds |\n| Rent burden | High commercial rent | Temple property (low/no rent) |\n| Operating days | Full week | Limited (3 days/week) |\n| Community | Novelty-seeking visitors | Temple parishioners + neighbors |\n| Revenue dependency | Cafe sales only | Multiple temple revenue streams |\n| Authenticity | Monks brought in | Natural extension of temple life |\n| Cost structure | High fixed costs | Low overhead |\n\n### Other Surviving Temple Cafe Examples\n\n- **Tsukiji Hongwanji Cafe Tsumugi**: Thriving since 2017 (see Section 3)\n- **Various shukubo tea/cafe services**: Integrated into temple stay experiences\n- **D&DEPARTMENT KYOTO d cafe**: Within Bukkoji Temple (see Section 4)\n\n### Average Revenue\n\nNot publicly reported for temple cafes specifically. The successful models tend to operate as cost centers that drive foot traffic and community engagement rather than standalone profit centers.\n\n---\n\n## 6. PlanetDAO\n\n### Investment Structure\n\n- **Minimum investment**: ¥34,540 (US$217)\n- **Model**: Co-ownership through DAO (Decentralized Autonomous Organization)\n- **Returns**:\n  1. Capital gains when reselling shares\n  2. Dividends from vacation rental business profits\n  3. Some properties offer free accommodation for shareholders above certain share thresholds\n- **Structure per property**: Each property held by a dedicated PlanetDAO company, supported by Planet Labs for structure and reporting\n- **Operations**: Local operating partners manage daily operations\n\n### Ryogonji Temple (了源寺) - First Property\n\n- **Age**: 170-year-old temple\n- **Location**: Irokawa district\n- **Status**: Registered Tangible Cultural Property (since 2021)\n- **How it started**: Local community introduced to PlanetDAO by Chief Monk seeking financial support\n- **Investment round**: 1,000 shares distributed\n- **Co-owners**: 134 investors from 15 countries\n- **Operations partner**: ShareWing Co., Ltd. (managing lodging facilities)\n\n### Sado Island Property - The Hojo Heritage\n\n- **Age**: 350-year-old compound\n- **Location**: Heart of Sado Island\n- **Buildings**: 5 traditional structures (Important Cultural Properties / Registered Tangible Cultural Properties)\n- **Architecture**: Blend of samurai, merchant, and rural styles spanning Edo and Meiji periods\n- **History**: Hojo family practiced traditional medicine for 7 generations\n- **Restoration estimate**: Main House alone: **¥600 million** (far beyond individual or even national support capacity)\n\n### Co-Owner Demographics\n\n- **Total countries represented**: 23 countries and counting (across all properties)\n- **Ryogonji specifically**: 134 co-owners from 15 countries\n- **Detailed nationality breakdown**: Not publicly disclosed\n\n### Legal Structure\n\n- **DAO framework**: Decentralized Autonomous Organization for governance/voting\n- **Real estate compliance**: Spent nearly a year developing legal and business structure compliant with Japan's strict real estate and securities laws\n- **Entity per property**: Each property held by dedicated company\n- **Governance**: Co-owners participate in voting on key decisions\n- **Founded**: 2023 by Tamaki Nishimura\n\n### Media Coverage & Partnerships\n\n- **South China Morning Post**: \"Abandoned Japanese temples are a great new tourism investment\"\n- **NHK Ohayo Kansai**: Featured Hokoji Temple restoration (4th project)\n- **Smart Money Asia**: Detailed feature on preservation model\n- **Ikigai Tribe podcast**: Episode 93 featuring founder\n- **POTLUCK AWARD 2025 finalist**: Organized by Mitsui Fudosan and NewsPicks (selected from 103 entries, top 12)\n- **Nachi Project**: Latest expansion (Substack feature by founder)\n\n### Founder\n- **Tamaki Nishimura**: Founded PlanetDAO 2023\n- Background: Previous inbound tourism agency that didn't survive COVID-19; discovered DAO concept as solution\n- Company: Planet Labs Inc.\n\n---\n\n## 7. Shinshoji Zen Museum & Gardens (Hiroshima)\n\n### Kohei Nawa x SANDWICH \"Kohtei\" Pavilion\n\n- **Completed**: 2016\n- **Designers**: Kohei Nawa (artist), Yoshitaka Lee and Yuichi Kodai (architects) from SANDWICH, Nawa's Kyoto-based creative studio\n- **Concept**: Ship-shaped building inspired by temple's founding to console spirits of those who died at sea (temple founded by shipbuilding company)\n- **Materials**: Three fundamental materials - wood, stone, water\n- **Exterior**: Kokerabuki (traditional wooden shingles), Japanese cypress cladding\n- **Structure**: Raised above landscape on two rows of pillars, creating sheltered space beneath\n- **Interior experience**: Meditation-like installation using water reflections and natural light\n- **Description**: \"An architecture that floats on waves surrounded by mountains\"\n\n### Temple Grounds\n\n- **Total area**: 230,000 m² (70,000 tsubo)\n- **Notable structures**:\n  - 17th-century temple hall relocated from Shiga Prefecture\n  - Reconstructed teahouse originally designed by Sen no Rikyu\n  - Temple office designed by Terunobu Fujimori (theme: red pine)\n\n### Zen Painting Collection\n\n- Collection exists but specific notable pieces and exhibition rotation details not publicly detailed in available sources\n\n### Zazen Experience\n\n- **Individual zazen**: ¥1,000 per person, advance reservation required\n- **Sutra copying**: ¥1,000 per person, no advance notice required\n- **One-Day Zen Experience**: ¥5,600 per person, advance reservation required\n  - Morning: Zen meditation\n  - Lunch: Yudame udon (warm udon with dipping sauce, served in Rinzai training hall style)\n  - Afternoon: Sutra copying\n  - Closing: Tea at one of the teahouses\n\n### Entry Fee\n\n- Adults: ¥1,800\n- University/high school students: ¥1,000\n- Junior high/elementary: ¥500\n\n### Workation Plan\n\n- Listed in navigation menu of official site, indicating the program exists\n- Specific details (pricing, duration, amenities) not publicly detailed\n\n### Hiroshima Architecture Exhibition 2025\n\n- **Event**: Inaugural Hiroshima Architecture Exhibition 2025\n- **Dates**: October 4 - November 30, 2025\n- **Format**: Triennial event\n- **Organizer**: Kambara & Tsuneishi Foundation\n- **Shinshoji's role**: Hosted \"Next Architecture: Connecting Futures Through Design\"\n- **Exhibitors at Shinshoji**: Sou Fujimoto, Junya Ishigami, Norihisa Kawashima, Hiroki Akiyoshi (VUILD), Clouds Architecture Office\n- **Special feature**: Preview exhibition of Kenzo Tange's self-designed 1953 Tokyo residence (planned reconstruction in Fukuyama), including 1/3-scale model\n- **Scope**: 23 architects and artists from Japan and abroad across Fukuyama and Onomichi\n\n### Annual Visitor Numbers\n\n- Not publicly reported. Described as attracting \"many Japanese interested in Zazen\" and a smaller percentage of foreign visitors\n\n---\n\n## 8. teamLab Temple Collaborations\n\n### Toji (東寺) Project - Kyoto\n\n- **Exhibition**: \"teamLab: Digitized World Heritage Site of Toji\"\n- **Dates**: August 6 - September 19, 2021\n- **Temple**: World Heritage Site, built 796\n- **Pricing**:\n  - Adults: ¥1,600 (weekdays) / ¥2,200 (weekends/holidays)\n  - Students: ¥600 (weekdays) / ¥800 (weekends/holidays)\n  - Preschoolers: Free\n- **Key installations**:\n  - Floating Resonating Lamps on gourd pond (autonomous, sound-responsive)\n  - Kodo flower cycle installation (interactive - flowers scatter when touched)\n- **Concept**: \"Digitized City\" - non-material digital technology turns buildings into art without physical alteration\n- **Visitor numbers**: Not specifically reported; teamLab Biovortex Kyoto (permanent museum) reached 500,000 visitors by February 2026\n\n### Engyoji (圓教寺) Project - Himeji\n\n- **Exhibition**: \"teamLab: Existence in Perception - Engyoji Temple\"\n- **Date**: Launched May 2023\n- **Location**: Jiki-do hall (Important Cultural Property, Muromachi period 1336-1573)\n- **Space**: 38m-long first floor hall transformed into artwork space\n- **Key works**:\n  - \"Massless Sun, Distorted Space\" - floating sphere of light that changes position as viewer moves\n  - \"Giant Solidified Spark\" - new installation\n- **Concept**: Questions the notion of existence\n- **Building**: Originally multi-purpose space where monks studied, slept, and ate\n\n### Kodaiji (高台寺) Projection Mapping - Kyoto\n\n- **History**: Seasonal illuminations 3 times per year (spring, summer, autumn)\n- **Temple**: Rinzai Zen temple, founded 1606 by Nene (widow of Hideyoshi Toyotomi)\n- **Technology**: Computer-generated 3D images projected onto architecture and landscapes\n- **Location**: Hojo (main hall), projected onto Hashintei karesansui (raked-sand garden) and gate\n- **Duration**: 3-minute dynamic show combining light, sound, historic architecture\n\n**2026 Spring Schedule:**\n- Dates: March 13 - May 6, 2026\n- Hours: 17:00-21:30 (gates close 22:00)\n- Admission: ¥800 (Kodaiji) / ¥500 (adjacent Entoku-in)\n- Theme: \"Eternal Bonds Spring - Nene and Matsu\" (friendship between two historical women)\n\n### Zuigan-ji x NAKED - Matsushima\n\n- **Event**: \"NAKED Matsushima - National Treasure Zuiganji Temple\"\n- **Note**: This is a **NAKED Inc.** project, not teamLab\n- **2025 dates**: October 24 - November 24, 2025\n- **Hours**: 17:00-21:00 (last admission 20:45)\n- **Theme**: \"Moon Viewing Light-Up\" - the moon that Date Masamune cherished\n- **Features**:\n  - Projection mapping in main hall front garden and kitchen (beams, arabesque carvings)\n  - NAKED Distance Lantern (participatory art)\n  - Special access to rooms normally off-limits\n- **Temple**: National Treasure, erected 1609 by Date Masamune\n- **Booking**: Available on Klook\n\n### Revenue Model\n\n- **General model**: teamLab charges admission, likely revenue-sharing with venue\n- **Specific splits**: Not publicly disclosed\n- **teamLab's approach**: Insists on ticketing model (proven since 2017 Silicon Valley show)\n- **Impact on regular visitors**: Temple installations typically run during off-hours or specific seasons, complementing rather than replacing regular visitation\n\n---\n\n## 9. TERA WORK / ShareWing\n\n### Company Overview\n\n- **Full name**: ShareWing Co., Ltd.\n- **Founded**: 2016\n- **Co-CEOs**: Naoko Unrinin and Koji Ueda\n- **Brand**: OTERA STAY\n- **Expansion**: Also launched \"JINJA STAY\" (shrine stays) - first at Mt. Oyama/Afuri Shrine, Isehara City\n\n### Number of Participating Temples\n\n- As of 2022: 11 temples\n- Growing to 13+ temples nationwide (recent data)\n- Over 20 affiliated temples mentioned in broader network\n\n### TERA WORK (Telework) Pricing Model\n\n**Initial launch locations (5 temples):**\n1. Shodenji Temple - Tokyo\n2. Zenkoji Temple - Gifu Prefecture (Takayama)\n3. Kanon-in Temple - Gunma Prefecture (Kiryu)\n4. Habanobo Shukubo - Yamanashi Prefecture (Minamikoma)\n5. Daitaiji Temple - Wakayama Prefecture (Shimowada)\n\n- **Concept**: \"Tera\" (temple) + \"tele\" (telework) = TERA WORK\n- **Model**: Temple grounds as shared workspace, combining inn services with Buddhist experiences\n- **Experiences included**: Zazen meditation, sutra copying, Buddhist teaching lectures\n\n### Temple Camp / RV Park\n\n- **Location**: Temple Hotel Daitaiji (Nachikatsuura, Wakayama) along Kumano Kodo\n- **Facilities**: Recreational Vehicle parks with power sources\n- **Options**: Camper-van stays, car camping sites\n- **Setting**: \"Pure air of Kumano Kodo\"\n\n### Cloud Hotel / Online Experience\n\n- Limited public information available\n- ShareWing focuses primarily on physical temple experiences\n- Recent launch: Well-being tourism program with Kouzakiji Temple (March 2025)\n\n### Corporate Training Packages\n\n- **Custom**: \"OTERA WORK\" service for corporate use (training camps, new employee training)\n- **Capacity**: Suitable for small groups to corporate retreats of 100+ people\n- **Example**: Hokoji Temple - limited to 1 group/day, max 70 people\n- **Pricing**: From ~¥100,000/person (varies by season and program)\n- **Process**: Concierge-style; ShareWing interviews guests about preferences, designs custom plans considering temple capabilities and client budget\n- **Digital nomad**: \"Digital Nomad Retreat\" program at temples along UNESCO Kumano Kodo\n\n---\n\n## 10. Temple Events / Community\n\n### World Heritage Temple Yoga Events\n\n- **Operator**: OTERA STAY (ShareWing) offers Zen Temple Yoga Retreats\n- **Setting**: Temples with deep history and tradition\n- **Format**: Yoga combined with Buddhist meditation practices\n- **Booking**: Through OTERA STAY platform and partner sites\n- **Specific pricing**: Varies by temple and program\n\n### Temple Matchmaking (寺コン / Tera-Kon)\n\n**How it works:**\n1. Singles are interviewed by temple staff\n2. Signed up to national network of matching agencies\n3. Temple arranges meetings with other singles\n4. Buddhist approach: emphasis on willingness to adapt to each other (not just shared values)\n\n**Key example - Tsukiji Hongwanji:**\n- Service: \"Tsukiji no Tera-Kon\" (築地の寺コン)\n- Launch: July (received 100+ inquiries since launch)\n- Philosophy: Monk Koshi Kimiya encourages \"open mind\" approach to marriage\n\n**Demographics:**\n- Male members: concentrated in 30s-40s, few above 50\n- Female members: span 20s-70s but concentrated in 30s-40s\n- Overall: more female than male members\n- Appeal factor: \"People feel comfortable turning to us because we are working for a temple\"\n\n**Context**: Japan's marriage rate dropped from ~10% (early 1970s) to 4.8% (2019)\n\n### Rakugo Performances in Temples\n\n**Example - Gangoji Temple (World Heritage):**\n- **Event**: \"World Heritage Night: Gangoji Yose\"\n- **Location**: Zen Room (usually non-public space)\n- **Content**: Professional rakugo and kodan performances\n- **Bonus**: Exclusive access to Gokurakudo Hall of Gangoji Temple for participants\n- **Format**: Traditional storytelling in historic Buddhist setting\n\n**Broader trend:**\n- English Rakugo Project launched (Tokyo Heritage Partners)\n- Performers like Ryusuitei Hanabi promoted internationally\n- Pricing: Typically ¥2,000-5,000 per performance (varies by venue)\n\n### Seasonal Events - Specific Examples\n\n1. **Kodaiji Illuminations**: 3x/year (spring, summer, autumn), ¥800 admission\n2. **NAKED Zuiganji**: Annual autumn event (Oct-Nov), nighttime access\n3. **Koyasan Okunoin Night Tour**: Year-round, ¥5,000\n4. **teamLab Toji**: Limited seasonal runs, ¥1,600-2,200\n5. **Ninnaji Moon Viewing**: Full moon nights, included in ¥1M stay\n6. **Tsukiji Hongwanji Pipe Organ Concert**: Monthly (last Friday), free\n7. **Shinshoji One-Day Zen**: Year-round by reservation, ¥5,600\n\n---\n\n## Cross-Cutting Insights\n\n### Revenue Model Spectrum\n\n| Model | Example | Price Point | Volume |\n|-------|---------|-------------|--------|\n| Ultra-luxury exclusive | Ninnaji | ¥1,000,000/night | 1 party/night |\n| Premium accommodation | Eko-in Gachirin | ~¥100,000+/night | Low |\n| Standard shukubo | Koyasan 51 temples | ¥15,000-50,000/night | High |\n| F&B + retail | Tsukiji Hongwanji | ¥2,200 breakfast | Very high |\n| Design retail | D&DEPARTMENT | ¥1,000-2,000 meals | Medium |\n| Micro-investment | PlanetDAO | ¥34,540 minimum | 134+ co-owners |\n| Art/event ticketing | teamLab/NAKED | ¥800-2,200 | High volume |\n| Corporate retreat | OTERA STAY | ¥100,000+/person | Group |\n\n### Success Factors\n\n1. **Authentic integration**: Most successful models embed commercial activity within genuine temple practice\n2. **Low overhead**: Using temple property eliminates major cost burden\n3. **Multiple revenue streams**: Temples that diversify (F&B + accommodation + events + retail) are more resilient\n4. **Story and meaning**: Every successful case has a compelling narrative (Amitabha's 18th vow, Kobo Daishi, Long Life Design)\n5. **Seasonal programming**: Adapting offerings to seasonal cycles (cherry blossom, autumn foliage, New Year)\n6. **International appeal**: Foreign visitor segment is growing and willing to pay premium for authentic experiences\n\n---\n\n*Sources compiled from web research across official sites, travel platforms, news outlets, and specialist publications. See inline references for specific data origins.*\n","numLines":758,"startLine":1,"totalLines":758}},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"32a1ef44-06b6-49a4-874e-5ffb30123384","sessionKind":"bg","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/Users/choi_ai/do-better-workspace","sessionId":"7ac17d9d-c157-4888-95a7-db59f2fd98e6","version":"2.1.220","gitBranch":"main"}
